6	\caption{Predictor relevance patterns across panel sizes. \textbf{(a)}~In small panels ($K{=}3$), a single vulnerable judge (red) can flip the majority; individual weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) is the stronger predictor ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$). \textbf{(b)}~As panel size increases, $\eta_{\max}$'s relevance decreases. \textbf{(c)}~At $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.286$--$0.353$) remains positive while $\eta_{\max}$ CI crosses zero. Bottom bars show relative predictive weight.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	The widespread adoption of multi-judge panels for LLM evaluation rests on an untested assumption: that aggregating diverse judges provides robust defense against adversarial manipulation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} showed that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities---position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy---that compromise any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}, motivating majority-vote aggregation inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}, reinforcing the belief that diversity is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.

24	\title{The Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes: Predictor Relevance\\Patterns in Multi-Judge LLM Evaluation under Adversarial Manipulation}
25	
26	\author{Anonymous}
27	
28	\renewcommand{\topfraction}{0.9}
29	\renewcommand{\bottomfraction}{0.9}
30	\renewcommand{\textfraction}{0.1}
31	\renewcommand{\floatpagefraction}{0.7}

33	\begin{document}
34	\maketitle
35	
36	\begin{abstract}
37	LLM judges are increasingly deployed in multi-judge panels, where diversity among judges is assumed to strengthen robustness against adversarial manipulation---an intuition grounded in Byzantine fault tolerance and the Condorcet Jury Theorem, both of which require independence. Recent work reports that 7-model committees can reduce attack success rates from 50--68\% to 10--19\%, yet the independence assumption is empirically violated: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, and panel accuracy saturates at moderate sizes. However, no existing analysis examines whether these gains relate to individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity---two properties structurally confounded ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) in the current model ecosystem, where expanding family coverage inevitably introduces weaker judges. We regress panel attack success rate on weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$) and a pairwise-MI diversity index ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. Two patterns emerge (direction consistent across metrics; significance metric-dependent): (1)~diversity and vulnerability covary positively (pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.286$--$0.353$, CI excludes zero), and (2)~$\eta_{\max}$ is significant at $K{=}3$ (pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$) but non-significant at $K{\geq}5$ ($0.091$, $0.086$; CI crosses zero). Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes composition effects ($R^2{\leq}0.17$). The diversity--capability confound precludes causal claims; all findings are associational\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cert_manip_resist_eval-588E/}.}.
38	\end{abstract}
39

43	\input{methodology}
44	\input{results}

47	
48	\section*{Limitations}
49	
50	\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between diversity and capability prevents causal separation; capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$).
51	\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is method-dependent (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 8/12, fractional logit 9/12); $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ $\kappa$-diversity show opposite $K$-trends.
52	\textbf{Scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; each attack uses 1--2 template variants; all API models were accessed via OpenRouter during March--April 2026 without version hashes.

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\subsection{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation}
4	
5	LLM judges gained traction after \citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy) and \citet{ye2024justice} provided a comprehensive bias taxonomy, motivating multi-judge panels, while \citet{raina2024judgerobust} and \citet{maloyan2025investigating} quantified adversarial vulnerabilities including prompt injection. Multi-judge aggregation extends the classical noisy annotator framework \citep{dawid1979maximum}: \citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias underlying within-family correlations. Most relevant, \citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\% to 10--19\% but did not separate diversity from individual robustness---the gap we address. Concurrent work benchmarks judge quality \citep{tan2024judgebench,li2024autoj} and judge--human agreement across tasks \citep{bavaresco2025llms}, adversarial risks in multi-agent systems \citep{tamas2025benchmarking}, and collaborative evaluation under benign conditions \citep{qian2026collabeval}; none tests whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.
6	
7	\subsection{Fault Tolerance, Voting Theory, and Ensemble Diversity}
8	
9	The Condorcet Jury Theorem \citep{condorcet1785essai} guarantees majority-vote convergence when voters are independent and better than chance; \citet{ladha1992condorcet} showed positive correlation weakens this guarantee. Classical BFT \citep{lamport1982byzantine} tolerates non-strategic faults but not adversaries targeting system structure; our framework adopts the Stackelberg model of \citet{tambe2011security} with the weakest-link principle \citep{schneier2000secrets}. Correlated LLM judge errors \citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate both CJT and BFT independence assumptions; \citet{kuai2026independent} propose DBEI (difficulty-weighted entanglement), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI. Mitigation strategies include Bayesian calibration and credibility scoring \citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.
10	
11	In ensemble theory, \citet{krogh1995neural} showed error decreases with diversity, but \citet{brown2005managing} demonstrated this holds only when members maintain low bias---violated when LLM panels include weak judges for family coverage. \citet{kuncheva2003measures} found no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicts improvement, which we replicate: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity yield opposite $K$-dependent trajectories. Our positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association under adversarial conditions instantiates Brown et al.'s caveat: diversity co-occurs with capability reduction, and the cost dominates. The position-bias exception (diversity \emph{reduces} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks) and the operationalization-dependent trajectory do not follow from classical theory. Recent frameworks \citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.
12	
13	\subsection{Adversarial Robustness of Evaluation Systems}
14	
15	Adversarial vulnerability \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial} and transferability \citep{papernot2016transferability} are well-studied in vision, where controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving}, diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving}, or vulnerability diversification \citep{zhu2020dverge} strengthens ensemble robustness---but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. In NLP, \citet{wallace2019universal} and \citet{zou2023universal} showed adversarial triggers transfer across models. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter, and the 15-judge combinatorial design induces correlated residuals addressed via wild bootstrap \citep{mackinnon2018wild} with max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:robustness_method}). Figure~\ref{fig:theory_comparison} (Appendix~\ref{app:theory_comparison}) summarizes the three assumption violations.
16	
```

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\subsection{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation}\n\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy) and \\citet{ye2024justice} provided a comprehensive bias taxonomy, motivating multi-judge panels, while \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} and \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} quantified adversarial vulnerabilities including prompt injection. Multi-judge aggregation extends the classical noisy annotator framework \\citep{dawid1979maximum}: \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias underlying within-family correlations. Most relevant, \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% but did not separate diversity from individual robustness---the gap we address. Concurrent work benchmarks judge quality \\citep{tan2024judgebench,li2024autoj} and judge--human agreement across tasks \\citep{bavaresco2025llms}, adversarial risks in multi-agent systems \\citep{tamas2025benchmarking}, and collaborative evaluation under benign conditions \\citep{qian2026collabeval}; none tests whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.\n\n\\subsection{Fault Tolerance, Voting Theory, and Ensemble Diversity}\n\nThe Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai} guarantees majority-vote convergence when voters are independent and better than chance; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} showed positive correlation weakens this guarantee. Classical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine} tolerates non-strategic faults but not adversaries targeting system structure; our framework adopts the Stackelberg model of \\citet{tambe2011security} with the weakest-link principle \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate both CJT and BFT independence assumptions; \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose DBEI (difficulty-weighted entanglement), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI. Mitigation strategies include Bayesian calibration and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\nIn ensemble theory, \\citet{krogh1995neural} showed error decreases with diversity, but \\citet{brown2005managing} demonstrated this holds only when members maintain low bias---violated when LLM panels include weak judges for family coverage. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicts improvement, which we replicate: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity yield opposite $K$-dependent trajectories. Our positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association under adversarial conditions instantiates Brown et al.'s caveat: diversity co-occurs with capability reduction, and the cost dominates. The position-bias exception (diversity \\emph{reduces} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks) and the operationalization-dependent trajectory do not follow from classical theory. Recent frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\subsection{Adversarial Robustness of Evaluation Systems}\n\nAdversarial vulnerability \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial} and transferability \\citep{papernot2016transferability} are well-studied in vision, where controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving}, diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving}, or vulnerability diversification \\citep{zhu2020dverge} strengthens ensemble robustness---but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. In NLP, \\citet{wallace2019universal} and \\citet{zou2023universal} showed adversarial triggers transfer across models. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter, and the 15-judge combinatorial design induces correlated residuals addressed via wild bootstrap \\citep{mackinnon2018wild} with max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:theory_comparison} (Appendix~\\ref{app:theory_comparison}) summarizes the three assumption violations.\n",

    }

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.78\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor relevance patterns across panel sizes. \\textbf{(a)}~In small panels ($K{=}3$), a single vulnerable judge (red) can flip the majority; individual weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) is the stronger predictor ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$). \\textbf{(b)}~As panel size increases, $\\eta_{\\max}$'s relevance decreases. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.286$--$0.353$) remains positive while $\\eta_{\\max}$ CI crosses zero. Bottom bars show relative predictive weight.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThe widespread adoption of multi-judge panels for LLM evaluation rests on an untested assumption: that aggregating diverse judges provides robust defense against adversarial manipulation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} showed that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities---position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy---that compromise any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}, motivating majority-vote aggregation inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}, reinforcing the belief that diversity is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nThis framing, however, conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them. Evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs rather than controlling nodes, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failures that violate the independence assumption underlying both classical fault tolerance and recent information-theoretic evaluation frameworks \\citep{schneier2000secrets,kaneko2025bits,turkmen2026ensemble}. Within-family error correlations substantially exceed cross-family values \\citep{kim2025correlated}, and panel accuracy saturates at moderate sizes \\citep{yang2026diversity}---suggesting that simply scaling panel size yields diminishing returns. More critically, diversity and capability are structurally confounded: expanding family coverage inevitably introduces weaker judges, so the protective effect attributed to diversity may instead reflect a capability cost. No existing work examines how these two factors relate across panel sizes.\n\nWe address this gap by regressing panel vulnerability on two predictors (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}): weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and a pairwise-MI diversity index ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks (MMLU, ARC) reveal two robust patterns.\n\nFirst, $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant at $K{=}3$ (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$) but becomes non-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.091$, $0.086$; CI crosses zero). Second, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with vulnerability persists across all panel sizes (pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero), though the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation---in 69\\% of conditions, the confound alone could account for the observed association. The pattern reverses for position bias, where diversity \\emph{reduces} vulnerability, consistent with the Condorcet intuition for shared-bias attacks.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association under confound.} Diversity and vulnerability covary positively (pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.286$--$0.353$, CI excludes zero), but both are confounded by capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$); the association partially survives capability-matched subsampling (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Position bias is the exception ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} < 0$; \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Weakest-link significance transition.} $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant at $K{=}3$ (pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$) but non-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ ($0.091$, $0.086$; CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero); significance trajectory is metric-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate variance explained ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",

    "newString": "Figure~\\ref{fig:theory_comparison} summarizes the three assumption violations.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\subsection{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation}\n\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy) and \\citet{ye2024justice} provided a comprehensive bias taxonomy, motivating multi-judge panels, while \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} and \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} quantified adversarial vulnerabilities including prompt injection. Multi-judge aggregation extends the classical noisy annotator framework \\citep{dawid1979maximum}: \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias underlying within-family correlations. Most relevant, \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% but did not separate diversity from individual robustness---the gap we address. Concurrent work benchmarks judge quality \\citep{tan2024judgebench,li2024autoj} and judge--human agreement across tasks \\citep{bavaresco2025llms}, adversarial risks in multi-agent systems \\citep{tamas2025benchmarking}, and collaborative evaluation under benign conditions \\citep{qian2026collabeval}; none tests whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.\n\n\\subsection{Fault Tolerance, Voting Theory, and Ensemble Diversity}\n\nThe Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai} guarantees majority-vote convergence when voters are independent and better than chance; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} showed positive correlation weakens this guarantee. Classical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine} tolerates non-strategic faults but not adversaries targeting system structure; our framework adopts the Stackelberg model of \\citet{tambe2011security} with the weakest-link principle \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate both CJT and BFT independence assumptions; \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose DBEI (difficulty-weighted entanglement), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI. Mitigation strategies include Bayesian calibration and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\nIn ensemble theory, \\citet{krogh1995neural} showed error decreases with diversity, but \\citet{brown2005managing} demonstrated this holds only when members maintain low bias---violated when LLM panels include weak judges for family coverage. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicts improvement, which we replicate: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity yield opposite $K$-dependent trajectories. Our positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association under adversarial conditions instantiates Brown et al.'s caveat: diversity co-occurs with capability reduction, and the cost dominates. The position-bias exception (diversity \\emph{reduces} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks) and the operationalization-dependent trajectory do not follow from classical theory. Recent frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\subsection{Adversarial Robustness of Evaluation Systems}\n\nAdversarial vulnerability \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial} and transferability \\citep{papernot2016transferability} are well-studied in vision, where controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving}, diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving}, or vulnerability diversification \\citep{zhu2020dverge} strengthens ensemble robustness---but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. In NLP, \\citet{wallace2019universal} and \\citet{zou2023universal} showed adversarial triggers transfer across models. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter, and the 15-judge combinatorial design induces correlated residuals addressed via wild bootstrap \\citep{mackinnon2018wild} with max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:theory_comparison} (Appendix~\\ref{app:theory_comparison}) summarizes the three assumption violations.\n",

In NLP, \\citet{wallace2019universal} and \\citet{zou2023universal} showed adversarial triggers transfer across models. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter, and the 15-judge combinatorial design induces correlated residuals addressed via wild bootstrap \\citep{mackinnon2018wild} with max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:theory_comparison} (Appendix~\\ref{app:theory_comparison}) summarizes the three assumption violations.",

```text
1	\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}
2	\label{sec:threat_model}
3	
4	\subsection{Panel Composition and Metrics}
5	
6	\begin{figure}[t]
7	\centering
8	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_theory_comparison.pdf}
9	\caption{Classical theory assumes independent voters (CJT), random faults (BFT), and beneficial diversity (ensemble theory). LLM judge panels violate all three: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, adaptive adversaries select optimal attacks, and diversity is structurally confounded with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$).}
10	\label{fig:theory_comparison}
11	\end{figure}
12	
13	\begin{table}[t]
14	\centering
15	\small
16	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
17	\begin{tabular}{llll}
18	\toprule
19	\textbf{Attack} & \textbf{Category} & \textbf{Mechanism} & \textbf{Target} \\
20	\midrule
21	Prompt inj.    & Targeted    & Override instruct. & Decision \\
22	Score manip.   & Targeted    & Numeric anchoring  & Score \\
23	Sycophancy     & Shared bias & Flattery/agreement & Preference \\
24	Verbosity      & Shared bias & Content padding    & Attention \\
25	Position bias  & Shared bias & Response reorder   & Position \\
26	Authority bias & Shared bias & Credential appeal  & Trust \\
27	\bottomrule
28	\end{tabular}}
29	\caption{Attack taxonomy. Targeted attacks exploit individual judge weaknesses; shared-bias attacks affect most judges similarly (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).}
30	\label{tab:attack_taxonomy}
31	\end{table}
32	
33	A \emph{panel} $\mathcal{P} = \{J_1, \ldots, J_K\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \in \{0,1\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\eta_{\max} = \max_j \eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \emph{panel attack success rate} is $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) = \Pr\!\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \geq t\bigr)$.
34	
35	To quantify diversity, we define a \emph{pairwise-MI diversity index} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ from mutual information among judges:
36	\begin{equation}
37	\label{eq:keff}
38	K_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K} \Bigl[1 + \sum_{j \neq i} \exp\!\bigl(-2 \cdot \mathrm{MI}(i,j)\bigr)\Bigr].
39	\end{equation}
40	This formulation aggregates pairwise MI directly without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\mathrm{MI} \to 0$) to $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\mathrm{MI} \to \infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \sim K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.
41	
42	\subsection{Adversary Model}
43	
44	\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]
45	\label{def:adversary}
46	A \emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\{\eta_j(\cdot)\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}) = \sup_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.
47	\end{definition}
48	
49	This departs from BFT models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.
50	
51	\paragraph{Weakest-Link Monotonicity.}
52	\label{thm:monotonicity}
53	Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones (formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}). This follows directly from the adversary model and is not a novel theoretical contribution. Stochastic dominance holds for 58\% of judge pairs (61/105), concentrating in cross-tier comparisons (79\% dominant) versus within-tier (28\%), predicting $\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\ref{eq:regression}. The relative importance of $\eta_{\max}$ versus $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
54	
55	
56	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\n\\subsection{Panel Composition and Metrics}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_theory_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Classical theory assumes independent voters (CJT), random faults (BFT), and beneficial diversity (ensemble theory). LLM judge panels violate all three: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, adaptive adversaries select optimal attacks, and diversity is structurally confounded with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$).}\n\\label{fig:theory_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{Category} & \\textbf{Mechanism} & \\textbf{Target} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPrompt inj.    & Targeted    & Override instruct. & Decision \\\\\nScore manip.   & Targeted    & Numeric anchoring  & Score \\\\\nSycophancy     & Shared bias & Flattery/agreement & Preference \\\\\nVerbosity      & Shared bias & Content padding    & Attention \\\\\nPosition bias  & Shared bias & Response reorder   & Position \\\\\nAuthority bias & Shared bias & Credential appeal  & Trust \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Attack taxonomy. Targeted attacks exploit individual judge weaknesses; shared-bias attacks affect most judges similarly (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\\label{tab:attack_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define a \\emph{pairwise-MI diversity index} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ from mutual information among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation aggregates pairwise MI directly without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.\n\n\\subsection{Adversary Model}\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from BFT models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \\emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.\n\n\\paragraph{Weakest-Link Monotonicity.}\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones (formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}). This follows directly from the adversary model and is not a novel theoretical contribution. Stochastic dominance holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105), concentrating in cross-tier comparisons (79\\% dominant) versus within-tier (28\\%), predicting $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\n",

          "+\\caption{Classical theory assumes independent voters (CJT), random faults (BFT), and beneficial diversity (ensemble theory). LLM judge panels violate all three: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, adaptive adversaries select optimal attacks, and diversity is structurally confounded with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$).}",
          "+\\label{fig:theory_comparison}",
          "+\\end{figure}",
          "+",
          " \\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]",
          " \\label{def:adversary}",
          " A \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },

```text
1	\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}
2	\label{sec:threat_model}
3	
4	\subsection{Panel Composition and Metrics}
5	
6	\begin{table}[t]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
10	\begin{tabular}{llll}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Attack} & \textbf{Category} & \textbf{Mechanism} & \textbf{Target} \\
13	\midrule
14	Prompt inj.    & Targeted    & Override instruct. & Decision \\
15	Score manip.   & Targeted    & Numeric anchoring  & Score \\
16	Sycophancy     & Shared bias & Flattery/agreement & Preference \\
17	Verbosity      & Shared bias & Content padding    & Attention \\
18	Position bias  & Shared bias & Response reorder   & Position \\
19	Authority bias & Shared bias & Credential appeal  & Trust \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\caption{Attack taxonomy. Targeted attacks exploit individual judge weaknesses; shared-bias attacks affect most judges similarly (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).}
23	\label{tab:attack_taxonomy}
24	\end{table}
25	
26	A \emph{panel} $\mathcal{P} = \{J_1, \ldots, J_K\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \in \{0,1\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\eta_{\max} = \max_j \eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \emph{panel attack success rate} is $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) = \Pr\!\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \geq t\bigr)$.
27	
28	To quantify diversity, we define a \emph{pairwise-MI diversity index} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ from mutual information among judges:
29	\begin{equation}
30	\label{eq:keff}
31	K_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K} \Bigl[1 + \sum_{j \neq i} \exp\!\bigl(-2 \cdot \mathrm{MI}(i,j)\bigr)\Bigr].
32	\end{equation}
33	This formulation aggregates pairwise MI directly without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\mathrm{MI} \to 0$) to $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\mathrm{MI} \to \infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \sim K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.
34	
35	\subsection{Adversary Model}
36	
37	\begin{figure}[t]
38	\centering
39	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_theory_comparison.pdf}
40	\caption{Classical theory assumes independent voters (CJT), random faults (BFT), and beneficial diversity (ensemble theory). LLM judge panels violate all three: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, adaptive adversaries select optimal attacks, and diversity is structurally confounded with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$).}
41	\label{fig:theory_comparison}
42	\end{figure}
43	
44	\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]
45	\label{def:adversary}
46	A \emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\{\eta_j(\cdot)\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}) = \sup_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.
47	\end{definition}
48	
49	This departs from BFT models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.
50	
51	\paragraph{Weakest-Link Monotonicity.}
52	\label{thm:monotonicity}
53	Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones (formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}). This follows directly from the adversary model and is not a novel theoretical contribution. Stochastic dominance holds for 58\% of judge pairs (61/105), concentrating in cross-tier comparisons (79\% dominant) versus within-tier (28\%), predicting $\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\ref{eq:regression}. The relative importance of $\eta_{\max}$ versus $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
54	
55	
56

    "oldString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_theory_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Classical theory assumes independent voters (CJT), random faults (BFT), and beneficial diversity (ensemble theory). LLM judge panels violate all three: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, adaptive adversaries select optimal attacks, and diversity is structurally confounded with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$).}\n\\label{fig:theory_comparison}\n\\end{figure}",
    "newString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ scatter at $K{=}3$ (455 panels). The $r{=}0.70$ correlation reflects structural coupling between diversity and capability in the current LLM ecosystem.}\n\\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter_main}\n\\end{figure}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\n\\subsection{Panel Composition and Metrics}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{Category} & \\textbf{Mechanism} & \\textbf{Target} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPrompt inj.    & Targeted    & Override instruct. & Decision \\\\\nScore manip.   & Targeted    & Numeric anchoring  & Score \\\\\nSycophancy     & Shared bias & Flattery/agreement & Preference \\\\\nVerbosity      & Shared bias & Content padding    & Attention \\\\\nPosition bias  & Shared bias & Response reorder   & Position \\\\\nAuthority bias & Shared bias & Credential appeal  & Trust \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Attack taxonomy. Targeted attacks exploit individual judge weaknesses; shared-bias attacks affect most judges similarly (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\\label{tab:attack_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define a \\emph{pairwise-MI diversity index} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ from mutual information among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation aggregates pairwise MI directly without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.\n\n\\subsection{Adversary Model}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_theory_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Classical theory assumes independent voters (CJT), random faults (BFT), and beneficial diversity (ensemble theory). LLM judge panels violate all three: shared pretraining induces correlated failures, adaptive adversaries select optimal attacks, and diversity is structurally confounded with capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$).}\n\\label{fig:theory_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from BFT models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \\emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.\n\n\\paragraph{Weakest-Link Monotonicity.}\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones (formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}). This follows directly from the adversary model and is not a novel theoretical contribution. Stochastic dominance holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105), concentrating in cross-tier comparisons (79\\% dominant) versus within-tier (28\\%), predicting $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\n",

At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ becomes non-significant.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental pipeline. 15 judges from 6 families are combined into panels at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, evaluated under 6 attack types across 2 benchmarks (12 conditions), yielding ${\\sim}$1.6M total judgments. Panel ASR is regressed on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity) and $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nSeparating diversity from capability requires panels that span the full range of both properties. We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers (Table~\\ref{tab:models}), ensuring that cross-family diversity varies independently of tier composition---a prerequisite for regression analysis. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX PRO~6000 Blackwell (96\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nWe study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Rather than sampling, we exhaustively enumerate all $\\binom{15}{K}$ combinations---455 at $K{=}3$, 3{,}003 at $K{=}5$, 6{,}435 at $K{=}7$---eliminating panel-selection bias and enabling exact computation of predictor statistics across every family-tier composition. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types spanning targeted and shared-bias categories (Table~\\ref{tab:attack_taxonomy}): prompt injection \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}, sycophancy \\citep{wang2023fair}, score manipulation, verbosity bias \\citep{zheng2023judging}, position bias \\citep{wang2023fair}, and authority bias. Each attack uses 1--2 prompt template variants; increasing template diversity is a priority for future work. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacks}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nOur regression requires two panel-level predictors that capture complementary failure modes: \\emph{individual weakness} and \\emph{structural dependence}. Per-judge vulnerability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$ is estimated empirically; $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the weakest link---the single judge most likely to be compromised, which determines panel failure at small $K$ where one flip suffices.\n\nPanel diversity is measured by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), a pairwise-MI diversity index that aggregates judge independence via $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ weighting (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_derivation}). We choose MI over agreement-based measures because MI captures all statistical dependence (not just linear correlation) and does not require a threshold for ``agreement.'' $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is used purely as an empirical predictor: the Gaussian copula from which it can be derived \\citep{nelsen2006copulas} is rejected by goodness-of-fit tests for 97--100\\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}), though panel-level rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean winner labels using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow correction. We also evaluate $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} as an alternative operationalization. The $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation is $r{=}0.70$ at $K{=}3$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:theory_comparison}; scatter in Appendix~\\ref{app:theory_comparison}), reflecting the structural coupling between diversity and capability: expanding family coverage inevitably introduces weaker judges.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo characterize the association between panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we need a model that captures multiplicative interactions: if doubling $\\eta_{\\max}$ multiplies ASR by some factor regardless of its level, the relationship is better described on a log scale than a linear one. We report log-log OLS as primary and fractional logit as co-primary; key conclusions require convergence across both. We fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction \\citep{benjamini1995controlling} for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} partitions $R^2$ into each predictor's incremental contribution. GAMs with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect varies between targeted and shared-bias attacks.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each judge appears in 91 panels), so residuals are correlated: panels sharing a weak judge will have correlated ASR values regardless of their other members, violating the OLS independence assumption. We apply three corrections: clustered SE \\citep[CR1, 3-position max-SE;][]{cameron2015practitioners}, mixed-effects models, and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check. Monte Carlo calibration confirms the bootstrap does not over-reject (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\subsection{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation}\n\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy) and \\citet{ye2024justice} provided a comprehensive bias taxonomy, motivating multi-judge panels, while \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} and \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} quantified adversarial vulnerabilities including prompt injection. Multi-judge aggregation extends the classical noisy annotator framework \\citep{dawid1979maximum}: \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias underlying within-family correlations. Most relevant, \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% but did not separate diversity from individual robustness---the gap we address. Concurrent work benchmarks judge quality \\citep{tan2024judgebench,li2024autoj} and judge--human agreement across tasks \\citep{bavaresco2025llms}, adversarial risks in multi-agent systems \\citep{tamas2025benchmarking}, and collaborative evaluation under benign conditions \\citep{qian2026collabeval}; none tests whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.\n\n\\subsection{Fault Tolerance, Voting Theory, and Ensemble Diversity}\n\nThe Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai} guarantees majority-vote convergence when voters are independent and better than chance; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} showed positive correlation weakens this guarantee. Classical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine} tolerates non-strategic faults but not adversaries targeting system structure; our framework adopts the Stackelberg model of \\citet{tambe2011security} with the weakest-link principle \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate both CJT and BFT independence assumptions; \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose DBEI (difficulty-weighted entanglement), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI. Mitigation strategies include Bayesian calibration and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\nIn ensemble theory, \\citet{krogh1995neural} showed error decreases with diversity, but \\citet{brown2005managing} demonstrated this holds only when members maintain low bias---violated when LLM panels include weak judges for family coverage. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicts improvement, which we replicate: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity yield opposite $K$-dependent trajectories. Our positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association under adversarial conditions instantiates Brown et al.'s caveat: diversity co-occurs with capability reduction, and the cost dominates. The position-bias exception (diversity \\emph{reduces} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks) and the operationalization-dependent trajectory do not follow from classical theory. Recent frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\subsection{Adversarial Robustness of Evaluation Systems}\n\nAdversarial vulnerability \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial} and transferability \\citep{papernot2016transferability} are well-studied in vision, where controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving}, diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving}, or vulnerability diversification \\citep{zhu2020dverge} strengthens ensemble robustness---but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. In NLP, \\citet{wallace2019universal} and \\citet{zou2023universal} showed adversarial triggers transfer across models. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter, and the 15-judge combinatorial design induces correlated residuals addressed via wild bootstrap \\citep{mackinnon2018wild} with max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:theory_comparison} summarizes the three assumption violations.\n",

```text
Now I have a complete picture. Let me compile all findings.

Here is a complete report of the data pipeline and all relevant files for `fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf`.

---

## Primary Figure Generation Script

**`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/scripts/gen_fig_keff_eta_scatter_fixed.py`**

This is the authoritative script for `fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf` (K=3, full pool, main text) and `fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf` (K=5, with Pool A overlay). It computes all scatter data on-the-fly — there is no pre-saved CSV or pickle of the 455-point scatter — by combining three data sources:

---

## Source Data Files (Inputs to the Script)

### 1. MI Matrix (K_eff values)

**`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/plan001/mi_matrix/mi_matrix_15models.json`**

Structure:
- `models`: list of 15 model IDs
- `mi_matrix`: dict with keys `mmlu`, `arc_challenge` — each a 15x15 matrix of pairwise MI values
- `keff_per_panel`: dict with keys `mmlu`, `arc_challenge` — each containing all 455 K=3 panel combinations, keyed as `"model_a|model_b|model_c"`, with per-panel fields:
  - `keff` (float) — the effective judge count
  - `models` (list of 3 model IDs)
  - `mi_pairs` (dict of pairwise MI values)

The script uses this to compute `K_eff` for every C(15,3)=455 panel by averaging the MI-based formula across both datasets.

### 2. Raw Judge Scores — Local Models (via checkpoints)

**`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/_taxonomy_checkpoint.json`**

Contains clean + attacked scores for the 6 local models across 3 attacks (verbosity_bias, position_bias, authority_bias). Structure:
- `clean_scores.{mmlu,arc_challenge}.{model_id}`: list of score dicts with fields `winner`, `score_a`, `score_b`, `reasoning`, `judge_id`
- `attacked_scores.{mmlu,arc_challenge}.{attack_name}.{model_id}`: same structure
- `pairs.{mmlu,arc_challenge}`: list of 300 evaluation pairs with `ground_truth_winner`

**`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/_step2v3_checkpoint.json`**

Contains clean + attacked scores for the same 6 local models across 3 attacks (prompt_injection, sycophancy, score_manipulation). Same structure as above.

Together these two checkpoints cover all 6 attacks × 6 local models:
- Local models: `qwen2.5-72b`, `llama3.1-70b`, `mistral-large`, `qwen2.5-32b`, `qwen2.5-14b`, `llama3.1-8b`

### 3. Raw Judge Scores — API Models (individual score files)

**`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/plan001/individual_scores/`**

210 files, one per `{model}__{dataset}__{attack}.json`. Models covered:
- `claude-3-haiku`, `claude-opus-4`, `claude-opus-4-6`, `claude-sonnet-4`, `claude-sonnet-4-6`
- `gemini-2.5-flash`, `gemini-2.5-pro`, `gemini-3.1-pro-preview`, `gemini-3.5-flash`
- `gpt-4.1`, `gpt-4.1-mini`, `gpt-4.1-nano`, `gpt-4o`, `gpt-4o-mini`, `gpt-5.5`

Each file has fields: `condition`, `scores` (list), `accuracy`, `n_samples`, `n_valid`, `n_errors`, `elapsed_s`. Each score entry has: `winner`, `score_a`, `score_b`, `reasoning`, `_parse_method`, `judge_id`, `latency_ms`, `input_tokens`, `output_tokens`.

---

## How the Scatter Data Is Computed

The script enumerates all C(15,3)=455 K=3 panels from 15 models. For each panel:
- **`K_eff`** (x-axis): `mean(mi_keff(mmlu), mi_keff(arc_challenge))` — averaged over both datasets using the formula `sum_i(1 + sum_{j≠i} exp(-2·MI[i,j])) / K`
- **`eta_max`** (y-axis): `max(model_avg_asr[m] for m in panel)` — the worst individual model's average ASR across all 12 conditions (2 datasets × 6 attacks)
- **`ASR`** per model: computed from individual score files; a flip is counted when the clean score is correct but the attacked score is wrong

The computed scatter is **not saved to any intermediate file**; the script produces only the output PDFs/PNGs.

---

## Output Files (the Figures)

- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf`** — K=3 main figure (455 panels, full pool only)
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.png`**
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf`** — K=5 appendix figure (with Pool A overlay)
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/plan001_step1_full_v4/chart/fig_keff_eta_scatter.png`** — earlier run output

A second, synthetic-data version exists at:
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/figures/gen_fig_keff_eta_scatter_v2.py`** — uses `np.random.seed(17)` to generate 455 correlated synthetic (keff, eta_max) points with `r_target=0.72`; this was used for an earlier "v2" figure and is not from real experiment data.

---

## Related Analysis Files (Aggregated Results, Not Raw Panel Data)

These files contain regression summaries and per-condition statistics, but not the raw 455-row panel table:

- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/results_step2v3_regression_20260522_031307.json`** — contains `all_20_panels` (only the 6 local models' C(6,3)=20 panels), with per-panel fields: `judge_ids`, `k_eff.{mmlu,arc_challenge}`, `asr_by_attack`, `eta_max_by_attack`
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/_project/data/per_condition_full_reanalysis.json`** — regression coefficients for K3/K5/K7 by condition (not raw panel scatter)
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/plan001/panel_regression/step4_regression_results.json`** — OLS, VIF, beta-regression summaries per condition, with `n_panels=455`
- **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/artifacts/server_backup/results/plan001/residualized_keff_15model.json`** — confirms correlation `corr_keff_eta ≈ 0.706` for mmlu×prompt_injection condition at 455 panels

---

## Key Column Summary

The scatter plot encodes these conceptual columns (computed on-the-fly, never saved as CSV):

| Column | Description | Source |
|--------|-------------|--------|
| `keff` | Effective judge count, averaged over mmlu and arc_challenge | `mi_matrix_15models.json` → MI formula |
| `eta_max` | Max individual model ASR across all 12 conditions | All 210 individual score files + 2 checkpoints |
| Panel = tuple of 3 model IDs | C(15,3) = 455 panels | enumerated in script |

No standalone CSV, pickle, or panel-level JSON with all three columns (keff, eta_max, and panel ID) exists in the project. The data lives entirely in the three source inputs listed above.

1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
2	"""Generate two K_eff vs eta_max scatter figures:
3	  - fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf: K=3, full pool only (for main text)
4	  - fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf: K=5, full pool + Pool A (for appendix)
5	"""
6	
7	import json
8	import itertools
9	import numpy as np
10	import matplotlib
11	matplotlib.use('Agg')
12	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
13	from pathlib import Path
14	from scipy.stats import pearsonr
15	from scipy import stats
16	
17	ROOT = Path("/root/cert_manip_resist_eval")
18	MI_PATH = ROOT / "artifacts/results/plan001/mi_matrix/mi_matrix_15models.json"
19	SCORES_DIR = ROOT / "artifacts/results/plan001/individual_scores"
20	CKPT_FILES = [
21	    ROOT / "artifacts/results/_taxonomy_checkpoint.json",
22	    ROOT / "artifacts/results/_step2v3_checkpoint.json",
23	]
24	OUTPUT_DIR = ROOT / "figures"
25	OUTPUT_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
26	
27	DATASETS = ["mmlu", "arc_challenge"]
28	ATTACKS = [
29	    "prompt_injection", "sycophancy", "score_manipulation",
30	    "verbosity_bias", "position_bias", "authority_bias",
31	]
32	LOCAL_SET = {"qwen2.5-72b", "llama3.1-70b", "mistral-large",
33	             "qwen2.5-32b", "qwen2.5-14b", "llama3.1-8b"}
34	
35	POOL_A_NAMES = [
36	    "gpt-4.1", "claude-sonnet-4-6", "gpt-4.1-mini",
37	    "llama3.1-70b", "mistral-large", "qwen2.5-32b",
38	    "gemini-3.5-flash", "qwen2.5-72b", "gpt-4.1-nano",
39	]
40	
41	# ── Load MI data ──
42	print("Loading MI data...")
43	mi_data = json.load(open(MI_PATH))
44	MODELS = mi_data["models"]
45	N = len(MODELS)
46	mi_matrices = {ds: np.array(mi_data["mi_matrix"][ds]) for ds in DATASETS}
47	model_idx = {m: i for i, m in enumerate(MODELS)}
48	
49	POOL_A_INDICES = sorted([model_idx[m] for m in POOL_A_NAMES])
50	POOL_A_SET = set(POOL_A_INDICES)
51	
52	# ── Load scores ──
53	print("Loading scores...")
54	
55	def _is_valid(s):
56	    if s.get("error") or s.get("format_error"):
57	        return False
58	    return s.get("winner") in ("A", "B", "tie")
59	
60	def _invert(scores):
61	    swap = {"A": "B", "B": "A", "tie": "tie"}
62	    return [{**s, "winner": swap.get(s.get("winner", "tie"), "tie")} for s in scores]
63	
64	clean = {ds: {} for ds in DATASETS}
65	attacked = {ds: {a: {} for a in ATTACKS} for ds in DATASETS}
66	pairs = {}
67	
68	for ckpt_path in CKPT_FILES:
69	    if not ckpt_path.exists():
70	        continue
71	    with open(ckpt_path) as f:
72	        data = json.load(f)
73	    if "pairs" in data and not pairs:
74	        for ds in DATASETS:
75	            if ds in data["pairs"]:
76	                pairs[ds] = data["pairs"][ds][:300]
77	    for ds in DATASETS:
78	        for mid, scores in data.get("clean_scores", {}).get(ds, {}).items():
79	            if mid not in clean[ds]:
80	                clean[ds][mid] = scores
81	        for atk in ATTACKS:
82	            for mid, scores in data.get("attacked_scores", {}).get(ds, {}).get(atk, {}).items():
83	                if mid not in attacked[ds][atk]:
84	                    if atk == "position_bias":
85	                        scores = _invert(scores)
86	                    attacked[ds][atk][mid] = scores
87	
88	for mid in MODELS:
89	    if mid in LOCAL_SET:
90	        continue
91	    for ds in DATASETS:
92	        fp = SCORES_DIR / f"{mid}__{ds}__clean.json"
93	        if fp.exists() and mid not in clean[ds]:
94	            clean[ds][mid] = json.load(open(fp)).get("scores", [])
95	        for atk in ATTACKS:
96	            fp = SCORES_DIR / f"{mid}__{ds}__{atk}.json"
97	            if fp.exists() and mid not in attacked[ds][atk]:
98	                scores = json.load(open(fp)).get("scores", [])
99	                if atk == "position_bias":
100	                    scores = _invert(scores)
101	                attacked[ds][atk][mid] = scores
102	
103	for ds in DATASETS:
104	    loaded = set(clean[ds].keys())
105	    missing = set(MODELS) - loaded
106	    print(f"  {ds} clean: {len(loaded)} models, missing: {missing}")
107	
108	# ── Compute individual ASR per model ──
109	print("Computing individual ASR per model...")
110	
111	def individual_asr(mid, ds, atk):
112	    if mid not in clean[ds] or mid not in attacked[ds][atk]:
113	        return None
114	    ps = pairs[ds]
115	    c_scores = clean[ds][mid]
116	    a_scores = attacked[ds][atk][mid]
117	    n = min(len(ps), len(c_scores), len(a_scores))
118	    if n == 0:
119	        return None
120	    n_correct = 0
121	    n_flips = 0
122	    for i in range(n):
123	        if not _is_valid(c_scores[i]) or not _is_valid(a_scores[i]):
124	            continue
125	        gt = ps[i]["ground_truth_winner"]
126	        if c_scores[i]["winner"] == gt:
127	            n_correct += 1
128	            if a_scores[i]["winner"] != gt:
129	                n_flips += 1
130	    if n_correct == 0:
131	        return 0.0
132	    return n_flips / n_correct
133	
134	model_avg_asr = {}
135	for mid in MODELS:
136	    asrs = []
137	    for ds in DATASETS:
138	        for atk in ATTACKS:
139	            a = individual_asr(mid, ds, atk)
140	            if a is not None:
141	                asrs.append(a)
142	    if asrs:
143	        model_avg_asr[mid] = np.mean(asrs)
144	        print(f"  {mid}: avg ASR = {model_avg_asr[mid]:.4f} ({len(asrs)} conditions)")
145	
146	# ── K_eff helper ──
147	def mi_keff(mi_mat, indices):
148	    K = len(indices)
149	    if K <= 1:
150	        return 1.0
151	    total = 0.0
152	    for i in indices:
153	        term = 1.0
154	        for j in indices:
155	            if i != j:
156	                term += np.exp(-2 * mi_mat[i, j])
157	        total += term
158	    return total / K
159	
160	# ── Build panel data for a given K ──
161	def build_panel_data(K):
162	    combos = list(itertools.combinations(range(N), K))
163	    full_keff, full_eta = [], []
164	    poola_keff, poola_eta = [], []
165	
166	    for combo in combos:
167	        jids = [MODELS[i] for i in combo]
168	        if not all(mid in model_avg_asr for mid in jids):
169	            continue
170	        ke = np.mean([mi_keff(mi_matrices[ds], list(combo)) for ds in DATASETS])
171	        eta = max(model_avg_asr[mid] for mid in jids)
172	        full_keff.append(ke)
173	        full_eta.append(eta)
174	        if all(i in POOL_A_SET for i in combo):
175	            poola_keff.append(ke)
176	            poola_eta.append(eta)
177	
178	    return (np.array(full_keff), np.array(full_eta),
179	            np.array(poola_keff), np.array(poola_eta))
180	
181	# ── Plot helper ──
182	def plot_scatter(ax, keff, eta, r_val, p_val, n_panels,
183	                 poola_keff=None, poola_eta=None, r_poola=None, p_poola=None, n_poola=None):
184	    ax.scatter(keff, eta,
185	               c='#999999', marker='o', s=8, alpha=0.3,
186	               linewidths=0, zorder=2, rasterized=True)
187	
188	    if poola_keff is not None and len(poola_keff) > 0:
189	        ax.scatter(poola_keff, poola_eta,
190	                   c='#e67e22', marker='D', s=20, alpha=0.5,
191	                   edgecolors='#d35400', linewidths=0.4, zorder=3,
192	                   label=f'Pool A ($r$={r_poola:.2f}, $n$={n_poola})')
193	
194	    # OLS regression line + 95% CI
195	    slope, intercept, _, _, se = stats.linregress(keff, eta)
196	    x_line = np.linspace(keff.min(), keff.max(), 200)
197	    y_line = slope * x_line + intercept
198	    ax.plot(x_line, y_line, color='#c0392b', linewidth=1.2, zorder=4)
199	
200	    # 95% CI band
201	    x_mean = keff.mean()
202	    n = len(keff)
203	    ss_x = np.sum((keff - x_mean)**2)
204	    t_crit = stats.t.ppf(0.975, n - 2)
205	    residuals = eta - (slope * keff + intercept)
206	    s_resid = np.sqrt(np.sum(residuals**2) / (n - 2))
207	    se_pred = s_resid * np.sqrt(1.0/n + (x_line - x_mean)**2 / ss_x)
208	    ax.fill_between(x_line, y_line - t_crit * se_pred, y_line + t_crit * se_pred,
209	                    color='#cccccc', alpha=0.4, zorder=1)
210	
211	    # Stats box
212	    stats_text = f'$r$={r_val:.2f}, $p$<0.001\n$n$={n_panels}'
213	    ax.text(0.97, 0.97, stats_text,
214	            transform=ax.transAxes, fontsize=7,
215	            verticalalignment='top', horizontalalignment='right',
216	            bbox=dict(boxstyle='round,pad=0.3', facecolor='white',
217	                      edgecolor='#cccccc', alpha=0.9))
218	
219	    ax.set_xlabel(r'$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$', fontsize=9)
220	    ax.set_ylabel(r'$\eta_{\max}$', fontsize=9)
221	    ax.spines["top"].set_visible(False)
222	    ax.spines["right"].set_visible(False)
223	    ax.tick_params(labelsize=8)
224	
225	# ── Global style ──
226	plt.rcParams.update({
227	    "font.family": "DejaVu Sans",
228	    "font.size": 9,
229	    "axes.linewidth": 0.6,
230	})
231	
232	# ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
233	# Figure A: K=3, full pool only → fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf
234	# ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
235	print("\n=== K=3 panels ===")
236	fk3, fe3, pk3, pe3 = build_panel_data(3)
237	r3_full, p3_full = pearsonr(fk3, fe3)
238	print(f"  Full pool: {len(fk3)} panels, r={r3_full:.4f}, p={p3_full:.2e}")
239	if len(pk3) > 2:
240	    r3_poola, p3_poola = pearsonr(pk3, pe3)
241	    print(f"  Pool A: {len(pk3)} panels, r={r3_poola:.4f}, p={p3_poola:.2e}")
242	
243	fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(3.3, 2.5))
244	plot_scatter(ax, fk3, fe3, r3_full, p3_full, len(fk3))
245	fig.tight_layout(pad=0.4)
246	for ext in ("pdf", "png"):
247	    fig.savefig(OUTPUT_DIR / f"fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.{ext}", dpi=300, bbox_inches="tight")
248	plt.close(fig)
249	print(f"  Saved fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf/png")
250	
251	# ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
252	# Figure B: K=5, full pool + Pool A → fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf
253	# ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
254	print("\n=== K=5 panels ===")
255	fk5, fe5, pk5, pe5 = build_panel_data(5)
256	r5_full, p5_full = pearsonr(fk5, fe5)
257	r5_poola, p5_poola = pearsonr(pk5, pe5)
258	print(f"  Full pool: {len(fk5)} panels, r={r5_full:.4f}, p={p5_full:.2e}")
259	print(f"  Pool A: {len(pk5)} panels, r={r5_poola:.4f}, p={p5_poola:.2e}")
260	
261	fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(3.3, 2.5))
262	plot_scatter(ax, fk5, fe5, r5_full, p5_full, len(fk5),
263	             poola_keff=pk5, poola_eta=pe5,
264	             r_poola=r5_poola, p_poola=p5_poola, n_poola=len(pk5))
265	ax.legend(loc='upper left', frameon=False, fontsize=7, markerscale=1.5)
266	fig.tight_layout(pad=0.4)
267	for ext in ("pdf", "png"):
268	    fig.savefig(OUTPUT_DIR / f"fig_keff_eta_scatter.{ext}", dpi=300, bbox_inches="tight")
269	plt.close(fig)
270	print(f"  Saved fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf/png")
271	
272	# ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
273	# Summary
274	# ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
275	print("\n=== VERIFICATION ===")
276	print(f"K=3 full pool: r={r3_full:.2f}, n={len(fk3)}")
277	print(f"K=5 full pool: r={r5_full:.2f}, n={len(fk5)}")
278	print(f"K=5 Pool A:    r={r5_poola:.2f}, n={len(pk5)}")
279	print("Done!")

    transform=ax.transAxes, fontsize=7, color='#555555',\n        va='top', ha='left')\n\n# Legend\nlegend_elements = [\n    Line2D([0], [0], marker=MARKERS[nl], color='w',\n           markerfacecolor=COLORS[nl], markersize=5,\n           markeredgecolor='white', markeredgewidth=0.3,\n           label=LABELS[nl])\n    for nl in [0, 1, 2, 3]\n]\nax.legend(handles=legend_elements, loc='lower right',\n          fontsize=5.5, frameon=True, fancybox=True,\n          framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='#CCCCCC',\n          handletextpad=0.3, borderpad=0.4)\n\nax.set_xlabel(r'$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity)', fontsize=9)\nax.set_ylabel(r'$\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability)', fontsize=9)\nax.spines[\"top\"].set_visible(False)\nax.spines[\"right\"].set_visible(False)\nax.tick_params(labelsize=7.5)\n\nfig.tight_layout(pad=0.4)\nfor ext in (\"pdf\", \"png\"):\n j:\n                term += np.exp(-2 * mi_mat[i, j])\n        total += term\n    return total / K\n\n# ── Build panel data (K=3) with tier info ──\nprint(\"\\n=== K=3 panels ===\")\nK = 3\ncombos = _source line 4057 · 2026-05-26T05:51:22.339Z_

[Director 指令] 重新调整主文 figure 分布。要求：**第 4-8 页每页恰好一张图，均位于页面顶端**。

执行步骤：
1. 先统计当前主文所有 figure 及其所在页码
2. 如果某些页有多张图，把多余的移走（移到其他缺图的页面或附录）
3. 如果第 4 页或第 8 页没有图，从附录中选一张重要且配色好看的图补上（优先选与该页文字内容相关的）
4. 调整每张 figure 在 .tex 中的位置，确保 LaTeX 浮动体落在目标页面顶部（`[t]`）
5. 需要时用 `\FloatBarrier` 或调整文字量控制 figure 的落点

最终效果：
- Page 1-3: 无 figure（或仅 Figure 1 全宽在 page 2）
- Page 4: 一张图，页面顶端
- Page 5: 一张图，页面顶端
- Page 6: 一张图，页面顶端
- Page 7: 一张图，页面顶端
- Page 8: 一张图，页面顶端

编译后用 `pdf_to_images` 验证每页图表分布，确认无误后告诉我路径。
