```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
5	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound could account for the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association in 25/36 conditions (69\%; Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}): $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ benchmark in only 11/36 conditions, concentrated in MMLU (9/11) and sycophancy/prompt-injection attacks (RV${\geq}0.30$). Pool~A capability matching ($r{=}0.50$) improves survival to 10/12 at $K{=}5$ (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}). Fractional logit confirms the positive association (9--12/12 across $K$), but establishing a causal relationship requires training-based diversity manipulation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).
6	
7	\paragraph{AWQ Quantization and the API-Only Pool.}
8	All six local models use INT4 quantization, which reduces accuracy by 5--30pp relative to provider-reported FP16 baselines (Table~\ref{tab:models}), placing them uniformly in the lightweight tier. This creates a compound confound: quantized models are simultaneously less capable and more diverse (cross-family), so the positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association may partly reflect quantization-induced vulnerability rather than diversity per se. Adding a binary quantization dummy ($D_{\mathrm{quant}}$) to the regression attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (from 8/12 to 6/12) but not at $K{\geq}5$ (Appendix~\ref{app:quant_dummy}). Restricting to 9 API models removes the quantization confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$): $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12); the API-only pool lacks statistical power (MDE $> 0.9$; Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}), meaning it cannot rule out a genuine diversity effect---it merely lacks the sample size to detect one. Conclusions about the diversity--vulnerability association therefore depend on the full pool, where quantization remains a potential confound.
9	
10	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
11	Aggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\ref{fig:aggregation_r2_heatmap}, Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.745$ at $K{=}3$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{\leq}0.17$ across all $K$). At $K{=}7$, Bayesian calibration fully neutralizes $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (0/12; Appendix~\ref{app:bayesian_entropy}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all 12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}), confirming that selecting judges by individual robustness dominates diversity-maximizing strategies regardless of panel size. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}), offering a deployable metric from clean-condition agreement alone.
12	
13	\paragraph{Metric Sensitivity.}
14	The diversity trajectory is operationalization-dependent: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains high ($8 \to 8 \to 7$/12) while $\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12) and Shannon MI shows a third pattern ($4 \to 4 \to 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_regime},~\ref{app:shannon_entropy}). Inference method compounds this: fractional logit with clustered SE shows an upward $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ trajectory ($9 \to 11 \to 12$/12), opposite to the bootstrap's decline ($8 \to 8 \to 7$). The $\eta_{\max}$ decline holds under all three operationalizations.
15	
```

1	\section{Experimental Methodology}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}
7	\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\eta_{\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\eta_{\max}$ becomes non-significant.}
8	\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\begin{figure}[t]
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_pipeline.pdf}
14	\caption{Experimental pipeline. 15 judges from 6 families are combined into panels at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, evaluated under 6 attack types across 2 benchmarks (12 conditions), yielding ${\sim}$1.6M total judgments. Panel ASR is regressed on $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity) and $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability).}
15	\label{fig:pipeline}
16	\end{figure}
17	
18	\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}
19	
20	Separating diversity from capability requires panels that span the full range of both properties. We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers (Table~\ref{tab:models}), ensuring that cross-family diversity varies independently of tier composition---a prerequisite for regression analysis. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX PRO~6000 Blackwell (96\,GB). API models are assigned to \emph{frontier}, \emph{mid-tier}, or \emph{lightweight} tiers following provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:models}).
```

0	\section{Results}
1	\label{sec:results}
2	
3	\begin{table}[t]
4	\centering
5	\small
6	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
7	\toprule
8	\textbf{Bench.} & \textbf{Attack} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Dom.} \\
9	\midrule
10	MMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
11	MMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
12	MMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
13	MMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
14	MMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & $\eta$ \\
15	MMLU & Authority bias & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
16	\addlinespace
17	ARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
18	ARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
19	ARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
20	ARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\

22	ARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
23	\bottomrule

25	\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}
26	\label{tab:dominance}
27	\end{table}
28	
29	\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}
30	\label{sec:dominance}
31	
32	Table~\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\ref{tab:models}).
33	
34	Under OLS, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 9/12 (exceptions: both score-manipulation conditions and MMLU $\times$ authority bias), consistent with weakest-link monotonicity (\S\ref{sec:threat_model}). Dominance analysis yields 7 co-dominant, 3 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 2 $\eta_{\max}$-dominant (both position-bias) conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}), but the $r{=}0.70$ correlation raises confound concerns (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
35	
36	Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares five correction methods (Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison}, Appendix~\ref{app:clustered_se}): $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with wild bootstrap at 8/12.     "oldString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ becomes non-significant.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]",
    "newString": "\\begin{figure}[t]",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ becomes non-significant.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental pipeline. 15 judges from 6 families are combined into panels at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, evaluated under 6 attack types across 2 benchmarks (12 conditions), yielding ${\\sim}$1.6M total judgments. Panel ASR is regressed on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity) and $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nSeparating diversity from capability requires panels that span the full range of both properties. We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers (Table~\\ref{tab:models}), ensuring that cross-family diversity varies independently of tier composition---a prerequisite for regression analysis. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX PRO~6000 Blackwell (96\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nWe study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Rather than sampling, we exhaustively enumerate all $\\binom{15}{K}$ combinations---455 at $K{=}3$, 3{,}003 at $K{=}5$, 6{,}435 at $K{=}7$---eliminating panel-selection bias and enabling exact computation of predictor statistics across every family-tier composition. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types spanning targeted and shared-bias categories (Table~\\ref{tab:attack_taxonomy}): prompt injection \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}, sycophancy \\citep{wang2023fair}, score manipulation, verbosity bias \\citep{zheng2023judging}, position bias \\citep{wang2023fair}, and authority bias. Each attack uses 1--2 prompt template variants; increasing template diversity is a priority for future work. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacks}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nOur regression requires two panel-level predictors that capture complementary failure modes: \\emph{individual weakness} and \\emph{structural dependence}. Per-judge vulnerability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$ is estimated empirically; $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the weakest link---the single judge most likely to be compromised, which determines panel failure at small $K$ where one flip suffices.\n\nPanel diversity is measured by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), a pairwise-MI diversity index that aggregates judge independence via $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ weighting (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_derivation}). We choose MI over agreement-based measures because MI captures all statistical dependence (not just linear correlation) and does not require a threshold for ``agreement.'' $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is used purely as an empirical predictor: the Gaussian copula from which it can be derived \\citep{nelsen2006copulas} is rejected by goodness-of-fit tests for 97--100\\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}), though panel-level rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean winner labels using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow correction. We also evaluate $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} as an alternative operationalization. The $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation is $r{=}0.70$ at $K{=}3$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter_main}), reflecting the structural coupling between diversity and capability: expanding family coverage inevitably introduces weaker judges.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo characterize the association between panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we need a model that captures multiplicative interactions: if doubling $\\eta_{\\max}$ multiplies ASR by some factor regardless of its level, the relationship is better described on a log scale than a linear one. We report log-log OLS as primary and fractional logit as co-primary; key conclusions require convergence across both. We fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction \\citep{benjamini1995controlling} for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} partitions $R^2$ into each predictor's incremental contribution. GAMs with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect varies between targeted and shared-bias attacks.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each judge appears in 91 panels), so residuals are correlated: panels sharing a weak judge will have correlated ASR values regardless of their other members, violating the OLS independence assumption. We apply three corrections: clustered SE \\citep[CR1, 3-position max-SE;][]{cameron2015practitioners}, mixed-effects models, and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check. Monte Carlo calibration confirms the bootstrap does not over-reject (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).\n",

API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Predictor Relevance Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11--12/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ is $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ but drops to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ and $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$, becoming non-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient reverses the BFT/Condorcet intuition: under random faults, independence helps (more independent judges $=$ lower joint failure); under adaptive adversaries, diverse panels expose a wider attack surface because the adversary can exploit heterogeneous vulnerabilities that do not overlap across judges.\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Heterogeneity}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association in 10/12 conditions with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor relevance across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (8--8--7/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.745$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{=}0.136$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 5+/1$-$). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 9/12 (exceptions: both score-manipulation conditions and MMLU $\\times$ authority bias), consistent with weakest-link monotonicity (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}). Dominance analysis yields 7 co-dominant, 3 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 2 $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant (both position-bias) conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_analysis}), but the $r{=}0.70$ correlation raises confound concerns (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares five correction methods (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}, Appendix~\\ref{app:clustered_se}): $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with wild bootstrap at 8/12. Positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods (MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} {=} 0.14$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Predictor Relevance Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11--12/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ is $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ but drops to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ and $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$, becoming non-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient reverses the BFT/Condorcet intuition: under random faults, independence helps (more independent judges $=$ lower joint failure); under adaptive adversaries, diverse panels expose a wider attack surface because the adversary can exploit heterogeneous vulnerabilities that do not overlap across judges.\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Heterogeneity}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association in 10/12 conditions with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor relevance across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (8--8--7/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.745$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{=}0.136$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 5+/1$-$). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

Positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods (MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} {=} 0.14$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Predictor Relevance Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11--12/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ is $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ but drops to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ and $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$, becoming non-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (CI crosses zero). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}). The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient reverses the BFT/Condorcet intuition: under random faults, independence helps (more independent judges $=$ lower joint failure); under adaptive adversaries, diverse panels expose a wider attack surface because the adversary can exploit heterogeneous vulnerabilities that do not overlap across judges.\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Heterogeneity}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association in 10/12 conditions with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor relevance across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (8--8--7/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.745$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{=}0.136$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 5+/1$-$). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_aggregation_r2_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Mean $R^2$ across aggregation methods and panel sizes. Threshold voting maintains high predictability ($R^2{=}0.745$ at $K{=}3$); Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes composition effects ($R^2{\\leq}0.17$).}\n\\label{fig:aggregation_r2_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_analysis}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound could account for the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association in 25/36 conditions (69\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}): $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ benchmark in only 11/36 conditions, concentrated in MMLU (9/11) and sycophancy/prompt-injection attacks (RV${\\geq}0.30$). Pool~A capability matching ($r{=}0.50$) improves survival to 10/12 at $K{=}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}). Fractional logit confirms the positive association (9--12/12 across $K$), but establishing a causal relationship requires training-based diversity manipulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{AWQ Quantization and the API-Only Pool.}\nAll six local models use INT4 quantization, which reduces accuracy by 5--30pp relative to provider-reported FP16 baselines (Table~\\ref{tab:models}), placing them uniformly in the lightweight tier. This creates a compound confound: quantized models are simultaneously less capable and more diverse (cross-family), so the positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association may partly reflect quantization-induced vulnerability rather than diversity per se. Adding a binary quantization dummy ($D_{\\mathrm{quant}}$) to the regression attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (from 8/12 to 6/12) but not at $K{\\geq}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:quant_dummy}). Restricting to 9 API models removes the quantization confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$): $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12); the API-only pool lacks statistical power (MDE $> 0.9$; Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}), meaning it cannot rule out a genuine diversity effect---it merely lacks the sample size to detect one. Conclusions about the diversity--vulnerability association therefore depend on the full pool, where quantization remains a potential confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\\ref{fig:aggregation_r2_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.745$ at $K{=}3$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{\\leq}0.17$ across all $K$). At $K{=}7$, Bayesian calibration fully neutralizes $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:bayesian_entropy}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all 12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}), confirming that selecting judges by individual robustness dominates diversity-maximizing strategies regardless of panel size. Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}), offering a deployable metric from clean-condition agreement alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Metric Sensitivity.}\nThe diversity trajectory is operationalization-dependent: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains high ($8 \\to 8 \\to 7$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12) and Shannon MI shows a third pattern ($4 \\to 4 \\to 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime},~\\ref{app:shannon_entropy}). Inference method compounds this: fractional logit with clustered SE shows an upward $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ trajectory ($9 \\to 11 \\to 12$/12), opposite to the bootstrap's decline ($8 \\to 8 \\to 7$). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under all three operationalizations.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01D81X7ZoqCYZYB8icNRvWLi`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experimental Methodology}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_pipeline.pdf}
7	\caption{Experimental pipeline. 15 judges from 6 families are combined into panels at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, evaluated under 6 attack types across 2 benchmarks (12 conditions), yielding ${\sim}$1.6M total judgments. Panel ASR is regressed on $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity) and $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability).}
8	\label{fig:pipeline}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}
12	
13	Separating diversity from capability requires panels that span the full range of both properties. We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers (Table~\ref{tab:models}), ensuring that cross-family diversity varies independently of tier composition---a prerequisite for regression analysis. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX PRO~6000 Blackwell (96\,GB). API models are assigned to \emph{frontier}, \emph{mid-tier}, or \emph{lightweight} tiers following provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:models}).
14	
15	We study three panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$). Rather than sampling, we exhaustively enumerate all $\binom{15}{K}$ combinations---455 at $K{=}3$, 3{,}003 at $K{=}5$, 6{,}435 at $K{=}7$---eliminating panel-selection bias and enabling exact computation of predictor statistics across every family-tier composition. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\times$ item $\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \S\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.
16	
17	\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}
18	
19	Two benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\ref{app:temperature}).
20	
21	Each benchmark is paired with six attack types spanning targeted and shared-bias categories (Table~\ref{tab:attack_taxonomy}): prompt injection \citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}, sycophancy \citep{wang2023fair}, score manipulation, verbosity bias \citep{zheng2023judging}, position bias \citep{wang2023fair}, and authority bias. Each attack uses 1--2 prompt template variants; increasing template diversity is a priority for future work. The 2 benchmarks $\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\ref{app:attacks}).
22	
23	\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}
24	
25	Our regression requires two panel-level predictors that capture complementary failure modes: \emph{individual weakness} and \emph{structural dependence}. Per-judge vulnerability $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$ is estimated empirically; $\eta_{\max}(a) = \max_{j \in \mathcal{P}} \eta_j(a)$ captures the weakest link---the single judge most likely to be compromised, which determines panel failure at small $K$ where one flip suffices.
26	
27	Panel diversity is measured by $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\ref{eq:keff}), a pairwise-MI diversity index that aggregates judge independence via $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ weighting (Appendix~\ref{app:keff_derivation}). We choose MI over agreement-based measures because MI captures all statistical dependence (not just linear correlation) and does not require a threshold for ``agreement.'' $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is used purely as an empirical predictor: the Gaussian copula from which it can be derived \citep{nelsen2006copulas} is rejected by goodness-of-fit tests for 97--100\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\ref{app:copula_gof}), though panel-level rankings agree closely ($\rho{\approx}0.86$). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean winner labels using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow correction. We also evaluate $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} as an alternative operationalization. The $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation is $r{=}0.70$ at $K{=}3$ (Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter_main}), reflecting the structural coupling between diversity and capability: expanding family coverage inevitably introduces weaker judges.
28	
29	\subsection{Aggregation Methods}
30	
31	Beyond majority vote ($\geq \lceil K/2 \rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \propto \mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\sum_j w_j \cdot \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\text{correct} \mid \mathbf{v}) \propto P(\text{correct}) \prod_j P(v_j \mid \text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).
32	
33	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
34	\label{sec:stat_framework}
35	
36	To characterize the association between panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we need a model that captures multiplicative interactions: if doubling $\eta_{\max}$ multiplies ASR by some factor regardless of its level, the relationship is better described on a log scale than a linear one. We report log-log OLS as primary and fractional logit as co-primary; key conclusions require convergence across both. We fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:
37	\begin{equation}
38	\label{eq:regression}
39	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon),
40	\end{equation}
41	with $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = \hat{\beta} \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\log x) / \mathrm{SD}(\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction \citep{benjamini1995controlling} for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.
42	
43	Dominance analysis \citep{budescu1993dominance} partitions $R^2$ into each predictor's incremental contribution. GAMs with smooth terms test whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect varies between targeted and shared-bias attacks.
44	
45	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
46	\label{sec:robustness_method}
47	
48	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each judge appears in 91 panels), so residuals are correlated: panels sharing a weak judge will have correlated ASR values regardless of their other members, violating the OLS independence assumption. We apply three corrections: clustered SE \citep[CR1, 3-position max-SE;][]{cameron2015practitioners}, mixed-effects models, and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check. Monte Carlo calibration confirms the bootstrap does not over-reject (max null rejection $1.6\%$ at $\alpha{=}5\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:monte_carlo}).
49	
```

Panel ASR is regressed on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity) and $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}",
    "newString": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental pipeline. 15 judges from 6 families are combined into panels at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, evaluated under 6 attack types across 2 benchmarks (12 conditions), yielding ${\\sim}$1.6M total judgments. Panel ASR is regressed on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity) and $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link vulnerability).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nSeparating diversity from capability requires panels that span the full range of both properties. We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers (Table~\\ref{tab:models}), ensuring that cross-family diversity varies independently of tier composition---a prerequisite for regression analysis. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX PRO~6000 Blackwell (96\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nWe study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Rather than sampling, we exhaustively enumerate all $\\binom{15}{K}$ combinations---455 at $K{=}3$, 3{,}003 at $K{=}5$, 6{,}435 at $K{=}7$---eliminating panel-selection bias and enabling exact computation of predictor statistics across every family-tier composition. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types spanning targeted and shared-bias categories (Table~\\ref{tab:attack_taxonomy}): prompt injection \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}, sycophancy \\citep{wang2023fair}, score manipulation, verbosity bias \\citep{zheng2023judging}, position bias \\citep{wang2023fair}, and authority bias. Each attack uses 1--2 prompt template variants; increasing template diversity is a priority for future work. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacks}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nOur regression requires two panel-level predictors that capture complementary failure modes: \\emph{individual weakness} and \\emph{structural dependence}. Per-judge vulnerability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$ is estimated empirically; $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the weakest link---the single judge most likely to be compromised, which determines panel failure at small $K$ where one flip suffices.\n\nPanel diversity is measured by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), a pairwise-MI diversity index that aggregates judge independence via $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ weighting (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_derivation}). We choose MI over agreement-based measures because MI captures all statistical dependence (not just linear correlation) and does not require a threshold for ``agreement.'' $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is used purely as an empirical predictor: the Gaussian copula from which it can be derived \\citep{nelsen2006copulas} is rejected by goodness-of-fit tests for 97--100\\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}), though panel-level rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean winner labels using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow correction. We also evaluate $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} as an alternative operationalization. The $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation is $r{=}0.70$ at $K{=}3$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter_main}), reflecting the structural coupling between diversity and capability: expanding family coverage inevitably introduces weaker judges.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo characterize the association between panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we need a model that captures multiplicative interactions: if doubling $\\eta_{\\max}$ multiplies ASR by some factor regardless of its level, the relationship is better described on a log scale than a linear one. We report log-log OLS as primary and fractional logit as co-primary; key conclusions require convergence across both. We fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction \\citep{benjamini1995controlling} for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} partitions $R^2$ into each predictor's incremental contribution. GAMs with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect varies between targeted and shared-bias attacks.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each judge appears in 91 panels), so residuals are correlated: panels sharing a weak judge will have correlated ASR values regardless of their other members, violating the OLS independence assumption. We apply three corrections: clustered SE \\citep[CR1, 3-position max-SE;][]{cameron2015practitioners}, mixed-effects models, and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check. Monte Carlo calibration confirms the bootstrap does not over-reject (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).\n",
